### Tool result

_source line 4035 · 2026-05-18T13:25:43.111Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01PmZwyZWWTvv71D2DweKKF7`</summary>

```text
50	Full (S2)
51	  & 0.97  & $\downarrow$94\%
52	  & \textbf{0} & \textbf{0}
53	  & 8.03
54	  & 8.03  & \textbf{31.90} \\
55	\bottomrule
56	\end{tabular}}%
57	\caption{Five-way ablation on SciERC (551 docs, Qwen3-8B, zero-shot).
58	$\Delta$DR: relative change vs.\ unconstrained.
59	Best per column in \textbf{bold} (CF excluded).
60	F1 $\times$100. Bootstrap: 10{,}000 iterations.}
61	\label{tab:scierc-ablation}
62	\end{table}
63	
64	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
65	%  (a) Core result
66	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
67	\paragraph{Core result: DR elimination at zero F1 cost.}
68	The full two-layer constraint configuration (S1) reduces domain/range
69	violations from 15.37\% to 0.93\%, a 94\% relative reduction,
70	% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093
71	while relation F1 moves from 8.23 to 8.36 ($+$0.13\,pp; bootstrap 95\% CI [$-$0.24, $+$0.58]\,pp, $p = 0.58$, 10{,}000 iterations).
72	% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0823; phase2_scierc_full_s1: R-F1=0.0836
73	% bootstrap: delta=+0.13pp, CI=[-0.24,+0.58], p=0.5824
74	The difference is not statistically significant ($p = 0.58$); constraint enforcement is F1-neutral on this dataset.
75	Post-hoc filtering achieves higher F1 in most zero-shot settings via precision gains from aggressive violation removal (SciERC: 8.63 vs.\ 8.36; DocRED: 9.61 vs.\ 8.23), but at the cost of discarding up to 48\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\ref{app:posthoc-extended}); OntoCoDec preserves recall by enforcing constraints during generation.
76	Per-relation DR drops to $\leq$2.82\% on all types, reaching 0\% on four of seven (Appendix~\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).
77	However, constraint enforcement elevates CF from 0.08\% to 9.90\% (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
78	
79	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
80	%  (b) Grammar layer
81	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
82	\paragraph{Grammar layer: cost-free structural guarantees.}
83	Grammar-only constraints eliminate RV (1.39\%\,$\to$\,0\%) and ET (0.40\%\,$\to$\,0\%) with no F1 impact, while DR remains unchanged (15.37\%\,$\to$\,14.84\%).
84	This configuration represents the capability ceiling of grammar-based constrained decoding~\cite{geng2023gcd}, which enforces JSON structure and vocabulary membership but cannot reason about cross-field semantic constraints.
85	% phase2_scierc_grammar_only: RV=0, ET=0, DR=0.1484, R-F1=0.0823
86	
87	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
88	%  (c) Enabling dependency
89	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
90	\paragraph{Enabling dependency.}
91	Semantic-only reduces DR by only 24\% (11.65\%) and cannot eliminate RV (1.51\%) or ET (0.40\%), which establishes the \emph{enabling dependency}: without grammar-enforced vocabulary, the semantic layer encounters OOV types with no domain/range rules.
92	Adding the grammar layer yields a further 92\% reduction (11.65\%\,$\to$\,0.93\%).
93	
94	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
95	%  (d) S1 vs S2
96	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
97	\paragraph{S1 vs.\ S2.}
98	S2 achieves comparable DR (0.97\%) but lower R-F1 (8.03 vs.\ 8.36) with fewer triples (1{,}644 vs.\ 1{,}729) and does not resolve the CF trade-off (8.03\% vs.\ 9.90\%); we adopt S1 as the primary configuration.
99	% phase2_scierc_full_s2: DR=0.0097, R-F1=0.0803, CF=0.0803, triples=1644
100	
101	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
102	%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning
103	%  exp_ids: lora_scierc_unconstrained, lora_scierc_full_s1
104	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
105	\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}
106	\label{sec:composability}
107	Table~\ref{tab:composability} tests composability across zero-shot vs.\ LoRA $\times$ unconstrained vs.\ S1 for Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B.
108	% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_unconstrained, phase2_scierc_full_s1,
109	%          w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,
110	%          llama_scierc_unconstrained, llama_scierc_full_s1,
111	%          w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval
112	
113	Fine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\% but leaves residual violations on tight-constraint relations (Table~\ref{tab:composability}).
114	Adding S1 eliminates these: Qwen DR 1.95\%\,$\to$\,0\% ($-$0.09\,pp R-F1), Llama DR 1.11\%\,$\to$\,0.09\% ($+$0.02\,pp).
115	On DocRED, the same pattern holds: Qwen DR 0.76\%\,$\to$\,0.01\% ($-$0.30\,pp), Llama DR 0.49\%\,$\to$\,0.20\% ($+$0.51\,pp).
116	% w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval: DR=0.01%, R-F1=40.56; w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval: DR=0.20%, R-F1=44.23
117	
118	Across all settings, fine-tuned + S1 yields DR\,$\leq$\,0.20\% with R-F1 within $\pm$0.6\,pp, which supports orthogonal composability.
119	Post-fine-tuning, grammar is a no-op; the semantic layer alone reduces DR by 39--72\% (Appendix~\ref{app:ft-ablation}).
120	CF interference drops from $+$9.82\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\,pp (fine-tuned), tracing to \textsc{Generic}$\leftrightarrow$X schema ambiguity (Appendix~\ref{app:cf-distribution}).
121	Beam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates residual DR and reduces CF to $<$1\% for both Qwen and Llama (Appendix~\ref{app:beam-search}).
122	
123	\begin{table}[t]
124	\centering
125	\small

127	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
128	\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrrr r@{}}
129	\toprule
130	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Setting} & \textbf{Mode}
131	  & \textbf{DR\,$\downarrow$} & \textbf{CF}
132	  & \textbf{R-F1\,$\uparrow$} & \textbf{E-F1\,$\uparrow$}
133	  & \textbf{$\Delta$DR} \\

Bootstrap: 10{,}000 iterations.}\n\\label{tab:scierc-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (a) Core result\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Core result: DR elimination at zero F1 cost.}\nThe full two-layer constraint configuration (S1) reduces domain/range\nviolations from 15.37\\% to 0.93\\%, a 94\\% relative reduction,\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: DR=0.1537; phase2_scierc_full_s1: DR=0.0093\nwhile relation F1 moves from 8.23 to 8.36 ($+$0.13\\,pp; bootstrap 95\\% CI [$-$0.24, $+$0.58]\\,pp, $p = 0.58$, 10{,}000 iterations).\n% phase2_scierc_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0823; phase2_scierc_full_s1: R-F1=0.0836\n% bootstrap: delta=+0.13pp, CI=[-0.24,+0.58], p=0.5824\nThe difference is not statistically significant ($p = 0.58$); constraint enforcement is F1-neutral on this dataset.\nPost-hoc filtering achieves higher F1 in most zero-shot settings via precision gains from aggressive violation removal (SciERC: 8.63 vs.\\ 8.36; DocRED: 9.61 vs.\\ 8.23), but at the cost of discarding up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives (Appendix~\\ref{app:posthoc-extended}); OntoCoDec preserves recall by enforcing constraints during generation.\nPer-relation DR drops to $\\leq$2.82\\% on all types, reaching 0\\% on four of seven (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-relation-landscape}).\nHowever, constraint enforcement elevates CF from 0.08\\% to 9.90\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (b) Grammar layer\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Grammar layer: cost-free structural guarantees.}\nGrammar-only constraints eliminate RV (1.39\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) and ET (0.40\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\%) with no F1 impact, while DR remains unchanged (15.37\\%\\,$\\to$\\,14.84\\%).\nThis configuration represents the capability ceiling of grammar-based constrained decoding~\\cite{geng2023gcd}, which enforces JSON structure and vocabulary membership but cannot reason about cross-field semantic constraints.\n% phase2_scierc_grammar_only: RV=0, ET=0, DR=0.1484, R-F1=0.0823\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (c) Enabling dependency\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Enabling dependency.}\nSemantic-only reduces DR by only 24\\% (11.65\\%) and cannot eliminate RV (1.51\\%) or ET (0.40\\%), which establishes the \\emph{enabling dependency}: without grammar-enforced vocabulary, the semantic layer encounters OOV types with no domain/range rules.\nAdding the grammar layer yields a further 92\\% reduction (11.65\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.93\\%).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) S1 vs S2\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{S1 vs.\\ S2.}\nS2 achieves comparable DR (0.97\\%) but lower R-F1 (8.03 vs.\\ 8.36) with fewer triples (1{,}644 vs.\\ 1{,}729) and does not resolve the CF trade-off (8.03\\% vs.\\ 9.90\\%); we adopt S1 as the primary configuration.\n% phase2_scierc_full_s2: DR=0.0097, R-F1=0.0803, CF=0.0803, triples=1644\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (e) Composability with Fine-Tuning\n%  exp_ids: lora_scierc_unconstrained, lora_scierc_full_s1\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Composability with fine-tuning.}\n\\label{sec:composability}\nTable~\\ref{tab:composability} tests composability across zero-shot vs.\\ LoRA $\\times$ unconstrained vs.\\ S1 for Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B.\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_unconstrained, phase2_scierc_full_s1,\n%          w5_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_lora_scierc_constrained_eval,\n%          llama_scierc_unconstrained, llama_scierc_full_s1,\n%          w5_llama_lora_scierc_unconstrained_eval, w5_llama_lora_scierc_constrained_eval\n\nFine-tuning alone reduces DR to 1--2\\% but leaves residual violations on tight-constraint relations (Table~\\ref{tab:composability}).\nAdding S1 eliminates these: Qwen DR 1.95\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0\\% ($-$0.09\\,pp R-F1), Llama DR 1.11\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.09\\% ($+$0.02\\,pp).\nOn DocRED, the same pattern holds: Qwen DR 0.76\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.01\\% ($-$0.30\\,pp), Llama DR 0.49\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.20\\% ($+$0.51\\,pp).\n% w5_qwen_docred_lora_constrained_eval: DR=0.01%, R-F1=40.56; w5_llama_docred_lora_constrained_eval: DR=0.20%, R-F1=44.23\n\nAcross all settings, fine-tuned + S1 yields DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.20\\% with R-F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp, which supports orthogonal composability.\nPost-fine-tuning, grammar is a no-op; the semantic layer alone reduces DR by 39--72\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:ft-ablation}).\nCF interference drops from $+$9.82\\,pp (zero-shot) to $+$1.18\\,pp (fine-tuned), tracing to \\textsc{Generic}$\\leftrightarrow$X schema ambiguity (Appendix~\\ref{app:cf-distribution}).\nBeam search ($k{=}4$) eliminates residual DR and reduces CF to $<$1\\% for both Qwen and Llama (Appendix~\\ref{app:beam-search}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lll rrrr r@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Setting} & \\textbf{Mode}\n  & \\textbf{DR\\,$\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{CF}\n  & \\textbf{R-F1\\,$\\uparrow$} & \\textbf{E-F1\\,$\\uparrow$}\n  & \\textbf{$\\Delta$DR} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Qwen3-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{Zero-shot}\n    & Unconstr.\\ & 15.37 & 0.08 & 8.23  & 31.01 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.93 & 9.90 & 8.36  & 30.98 & $\\downarrow$94\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{LoRA}\n    & Unconstr.\\ &  1.95 & 0    & 43.86 & 67.05 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    & \\textbf{0} & 1.18 & 43.77 & 67.05 & $\\downarrow$100\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{Llama-3.1-8B}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{Zero-shot}\n    & Unconstr.\\ & 13.34 & 0.24 & 2.68  & 23.56 & --- \\\\\n  & & Full S1    &  0.08 & 6.56 & 2.54  & 23.49 & $\\downarrow$99\\% \\\\\n\\cmidrule(l){2-8}\n  & \\multirow{2}{*}{LoRA}",
      "numLines": 100,
      "startLine": 50,
      "totalLines": 160
    }
  },

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/discussion_paragraphs_cf83d1.tex:15:In our evaluation, F1 serves as a \emph{control variable} verifying that constraint enforcement does not degrade extraction quality, not as an optimization target.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/discussion_paragraphs_cf83d1.tex:23:Domain/range (DR) violations produce structurally invalid triples---a triple asserting that a \emph{Task} entity fills the \emph{domain} slot of \textsc{Part-of}, which requires \emph{Method}, cannot be consumed by any downstream system without error.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/discussion_paragraphs_cf83d1.tex:43:The grammar layer eliminates these OOV predictions, removing a substantial source of false positives and yielding a statistically significant precision gain.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_doc_length.tex:67:Longer documents produce more violations in the unconstrained setting (e.g., DocRED long-bin: 3,550 vs.\ short-bin: 2,445), yet OntoCoDec achieves proportionally similar reduction, confirming that the constraint framework scales reliably with document complexity.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:11:LMQL~\cite{beurer-kellner2023lmql} introduces a declarative query language that compiles type, length, and regex constraints into token-level masks, providing a programmable interface for specifying multi-variable output structures.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:14:Together, these advances have made grammar-constrained decoding both fast and statistically principled, establishing a robust foundation for structured generation.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:22:In programming-language generation, constrained decoding has been extended beyond syntax to enforce semantic properties such as type correctness, demonstrating that richer-than-CFG constraints can be compiled into token-level filtering.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:25:In contrast, OntoCoDec's entity registry is populated \emph{during} generation as entity spans are extracted from free text, requiring constraint sets that evolve with the decoding prefix, a fundamental distinction between querying a known schema and extracting from unstructured text.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:26:Synchromesh~\cite{synchromesh_iclr2022} pioneers Constrained Semantic Decoding (CSD), which enforces syntax, type, and scope constraints simultaneously during code generation from pre-trained LMs without fine-tuning, establishing that semantic constraints can be expressed as dynamic token masks conditioned on the generation prefix.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:27:MGD~\cite{mgd_neurips2023} uses static-analysis monitors to perform incremental type checking at decode time, enabling SantaCoder-1.1B to outperform text-davinci-003 on repository-level code completion by ensuring that every generated identifier is type-correct with respect to the surrounding code context.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:31:Conversely, IE introduces a challenge absent in code generation: the constraint target (entity type) must be inferred from free-text spans rather than read from declared type annotations, requiring a stateful entity registry that tracks extracted types during autoregressive generation.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:43:AdaKGC~\cite{adakgc_emnlp2023} introduces schema-constrained dynamic decoding with trie-based prefix trees that adapt to the target schema, enforcing that generated entity and relation names belong to a predefined inventory, a vocabulary-level constraint that ensures name validity but does not verify type compatibility between a relation and its arguments.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:45:NeuroLogic Decoding~\cite{lu2021neurologic} expresses lexical inclusion and exclusion constraints as predicate logic formulas enforced during beam search, offering a general mechanism for token-level control that can encode vocabulary membership but not relational type rules.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:57:KnowCoder~\cite{knowcoder_acl2024} takes this further by encoding 29K entity types from Wikidata as a Python class hierarchy, training a Code-LLM to generate structured extraction code that implicitly respects type inheritance, the most comprehensive schema-as-code approach to date.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:71:Rather than constraining generation, several methods validate LLM outputs against ontological rules after generation, applying a detect-then-fix paradigm.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_related_work.tex:92:\citet{tam2024letmespeak} conduct a comprehensive evaluation showing that rigid format constraints (JSON, XML) harm performance on math and multi-hop QA benchmarks but \emph{benefit} classification-style tasks such as slot filling, a distinction directly relevant to IE, which is fundamentally an extraction (classification-like) task rather than a reasoning task.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/section3_method_draft.tex:46:This schema is compiled into an EBNF grammar via XGrammar~\cite{dong2025xgrammar}, producing a pushdown automaton tracking JSON structure and enum membership.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_discussion.tex:36:This metric directly quantifies the enabling dependency (\S\ref{sec:interaction}): it measures the fraction of decoding steps where both constraint layers are jointly active, providing a fine-grained view beyond aggregate violation rates.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_discussion.tex:70:The grammar-first fallback is triggered at 36.64\% of type-bearing positions, indicating that this conflict is structural, not a tunable hyperparameter.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_discussion.tex:95:Mistral-7B (unconstrained ET${=}$7.58\% on DocRED) deviates from this pattern: its residual DR on DocRED (33.21\%) substantially exceeds the linear prediction, confirming that when model calibration is insufficient (ET${>}$5\% on either dataset), residual DR is dominated by entity misclassification rather than constraint-search failure.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_discussion.tex:115:Llama-3.1-8B's lower baseline RV (16.03\%) yields a smaller but directionally consistent improvement ($+$0.29\,pp vs.\ $+$0.50\,pp), confirming that F1 gain magnitude is proportional to baseline OOV rate.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_discussion.tex:129:In our evaluation, F1 serves as a \emph{control variable} verifying that constraint enforcement does not degrade extraction quality, not as an optimization target.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_discussion.tex:159:Under unconstrained decoding, CF rates collapse to $\leq$1\% on both datasets, confirming that CF violations are a side-effect of per-slot type commitment rather than a model deficiency.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_discussion.tex:275:Adding both layers achieves near-complete elimination (DR\,$\leq$\,0.20\% in all 4 settings), confirming that even after fine-tuning, the semantic layer's pair-based enforcement captures violations that training alone cannot eliminate, specifically on tight-constraint relations where the valid type-pair space is small.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_discussion.tex:288:\item \emph{Entity type errors} (39\%): the LLM assigns an incorrect entity type (e.g., labeling a geographic entity as \textsc{LOC} when the gold standard requires \textsc{MISC}), causing the semantic mask to permit a domain/range-incompatible relation.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_6/projects/schema_semantic_constrained_ie/artifacts/_project/paper/appendix_discussion.tex:298:The implication is twofold: (i)~the irreducible floor from schema gaps is small ($\sim$3 violations across 4 settings), confirming that the constraint system operates near its theoretical optimum; (ii)~residual violations from entity typing errors are addressable through stronger base models or improved entity typing modules, without modifying the constraint architecture.

58	% exp_ids: phase2_docred_unconstrained, phase2_docred_full_s1
59	\paragraph{Core result.}
60	Full S1 reduces DR from 18.46\% to 10.21\% ($\downarrow$45\%), eliminates ET ($\to$\,0\%), and near-eliminates RV (29.66\%\,$\to$\,0.03\%).
61	R-F1 improves from 7.74 to 8.23 ($+$0.50\,pp; $p < 0.001$), attributable to elimination of 5{,}189 OOV relation predictions.
62	CF increases modestly (0.10\%\,$\to$\,2.57\%), consistent with the CF$\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
63	
64	% exp_ids: phase2_docred_grammar_only
65	\paragraph{Grammar layer surfaces latent violations.}
66	Grammar-only constraints eliminate RV and ET but \emph{increase} DR to 26.17\% ($+$42\%): the baseline's 29.66\% OOV triples cannot be evaluated for DR; grammar enforcement assigns valid relation names whose domain/range rules the associated types frequently violate, \emph{revealing} latent incompatibilities that motivate the semantic layer.
67	
68	% exp_ids: phase2_docred_semantic_only, phase2_docred_full_s1
69	\paragraph{Enabling dependency and tightness ceiling.}
70	Semantic-only reduces DR by 25\% but cannot eliminate RV (29.87\%) or prevent ET increases (2.48\%\,$\to$\,4.22\%), which replicates SciERC's enabling dependency.
71	Residual DR (10.21\%) exceeds SciERC's (0.93\%) due to higher constraint tightness ($\bar{\kappa}{=}0.842$ vs.\ 0.333): the grammar-first fallback rate reaches 10.2\% of triples (effective semantic coverage 89.8\%), and the near-identity with residual DR indicates that violations originate almost exclusively from fallback events (Appendix~\ref{app:fallback-coverage}).
72

````text
No exit files for the 70B SciERC experiments. But the processes have exited (PID 24552 and 24703 are gone, GPUs at 0%). 

The exit file is created by the tmux wrapper's EXIT trap:
```
trap '_EC=$?; echo "EXIT_CODE=$_EC" >> log; printf "%s|%s\n" "$_EC" "$(date)" > /tmp/agent-ml-exit/{name}.exit' EXIT
```

Wait, this is on westd-21461. Let me check if the trap was set for these 70B SciERC experiments. 

Looking at the registry, these experiments were submitted directly with `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0 python run_constrained_extraction.py ...` rather than through the submit_training_job wrapper, so there wouldn't be an exit trap. The logs are sitting in /tmp/ rather than ~/runs/, which suggests they were started as direct processes without the standard job infrastructure.

The DocRED 70B experiments do use submit_training_job with proper exit files, but the SciERC 70B work on westd-21461 was launched manually by a Worker outside that framework. I can still verify completion by checking the output file line count, and I've already notified Director about the results. At this point I've covered the main audit items — the 70B SciERC completion is verified and reported.

Now I'm waiting for the next phase: the Agent processing SciERC results with violation checking and metric registration, DocRED finishing around 22:45 UTC, and any new Worker or Paper Agent updates. I want to look at the 70B SciERC unconstrained output to see what violation types appear and whether the 70B model shows notably lower DR than 8B, which would be relevant to the model scaling claim.

Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps.         "content": "% Section 5 — DocRED Results (compressed, 1450→~600 words)\n% exp_ids: phase2_docred_unconstrained, phase2_docred_grammar_only,\n%          phase2_docred_semantic_only, phase2_docred_full_s1\n% All numbers verified against registry 2026-05-14.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Dataset Validation on DocRED}\n\\label{sec:docred-results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:docred-ablation} presents the four-way ablation on DocRED (96 Wikidata relation types, 14$\\times$ SciERC; Qwen3-8B, zero-shot, 998 documents).\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  Table: DocRED four-way ablation\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.6pt}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrrr rr@{}}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{5}{c}{\\textbf{Violation Rates (\\%)}}\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{F1}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-6} \\cmidrule(l){7-8}\n\\textbf{Mode}\n  & DR\\,$\\downarrow$ & $\\Delta$DR\n  & RV\\,$\\downarrow$ & ET\\,$\\downarrow$ & CF\n  & Rel.\\,$\\uparrow$ & Ent.\\,$\\uparrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nUnconstrained\n  & 18.46 & ---\n  & 29.66 & 2.48\n  & 0.10\n  & 7.74  & 58.15 \\\\\nGrammar\n  & 26.17 & $\\uparrow$42\\%\n  & \\textbf{0.03} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 0.07\n  & \\textbf{8.31}  & \\textbf{59.38} \\\\\nSemantic\n  & 13.84 & $\\downarrow$25\\%\n  & 29.87 & 4.22\n  & 1.05\n  & 7.70  & 57.81 \\\\\nFull (S1)\n  & \\textbf{10.21} & $\\downarrow$\\textbf{45\\%}\n  & \\textbf{0.03} & \\textbf{0}\n  & 2.57\n  & 8.23  & 59.37 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}%\n\\caption{Four-way ablation on DocRED (998 documents, Qwen3-8B, zero-shot).\n$\\Delta$DR: relative change vs.\\ unconstrained.\nBest per column in \\textbf{bold} (CF excluded).\nF1 $\\times$100. Bootstrap significance: R-F1 $+$0.50\\,pp ($p < 0.001$).}\n\\label{tab:docred-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\n% exp_ids: phase2_docred_unconstrained, phase2_docred_full_s1\n\\paragraph{Core result.}\nFull S1 reduces DR from 18.46\\% to 10.21\\% ($\\downarrow$45\\%), eliminates ET ($\\to$\\,0\\%), and near-eliminates RV (29.66\\%\\,$\\to$\\,0.03\\%).\nR-F1 improves from 7.74 to 8.23 ($+$0.50\\,pp; $p < 0.001$), attributable to elimination of 5{,}189 OOV relation predictions.\nCF increases modestly (0.10\\%\\,$\\to$\\,2.57\\%), consistent with the CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n% exp_ids: phase2_docred_grammar_only\n\\paragraph{Grammar layer surfaces latent violations.}\nGrammar-only constraints eliminate RV and ET but \\emph{increase} DR to 26.17\\% ($+$42\\%): the baseline's 29.66\\% OOV triples cannot be evaluated for DR; grammar enforcement assigns valid relation names whose domain/range rules the associated types frequently violate, \\emph{revealing} latent incompatibilities that motivate the semantic layer.\n\n% exp_ids: phase2_docred_semantic_only, phase2_docred_full_s1\n\\paragraph{Enabling dependency and tightness ceiling.}\nSemantic-only reduces DR by 25\\% but cannot eliminate RV (29.87\\%) or prevent ET increases (2.48\\%\\,$\\to$\\,4.22\\%), which replicates SciERC's enabling dependency.\nResidual DR (10.21\\%) exceeds SciERC's (0.93\\%) due to higher constraint tightness ($\\bar{\\kappa}{=}0.842$ vs.\\ 0.333): residual DR of 10.21\\% implies effective semantic coverage of approximately 90\\%, consistent with tight constraints forcing frequent grammar-first fallback (Appendix~\\ref{app:fallback-coverage}).\n",

        "content": "% Section 1: Introduction (compressed, 669→~550 words)\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge language models are increasingly deployed for zero-shot information extraction~\\cite{li2023codeie,sainz2024gollie,wei2023zeroshot}, but when generating relation triples $(h, r, t)$, neither entity is guaranteed to carry a type consistent with the ontology's domain/range constraints for $r$.\nWe call such outputs \\emph{domain/range violations}: structurally valid triples that are ontologically invalid.\nIn a systematic study, we find violations are pervasive: 15.4\\% of triples on SciERC~\\cite{luan2018scierc} and 18.5\\% on DocRED~\\cite{yao2019docred}, driven by \\emph{constraint tightness} (few valid type pairs per relation) rather than ontology scale.\nRelations with tight constraints exhibit far higher rates (\\textsc{Compare}: 42.4\\%) than loosely constrained ones (\\textsc{Used-for}: 0.8\\%).\n\nExisting approaches leave a fundamental gap.\nSchema-informed prompting reduces violations by 62\\% on average but is per-relation unreliable (violations for \\textsc{Evaluate-for} actually \\emph{increase} by 5.8\\,pp).\nGrammar-constrained decoding~\\cite{dong2025xgrammar,willard2023outlines,beurer-kellner2023lmql} guarantees syntactic well-formedness but cannot encode cross-field semantic constraints.\nPost-hoc filtering achieves higher F1 in most zero-shot settings via precision gains, but at the cost of discarding up to 48\\% of predictions including true positives; at high rejection rates (e.g., Mistral DocRED 73.7\\%), it discards the majority of outputs, making it impractical.\nOntoCoDec preserves recall by enforcing constraints during generation rather than discarding non-compliant outputs.\nTraining-based approaches~\\cite{sainz2024gollie,lu2022uie} provide only soft guidance that cannot eliminate violations.\n\nWe propose \\textbf{OntoCoDec} (\\textbf{Onto}logy-\\textbf{Co}mpiled \\textbf{Dec}oding), a framework that compiles an IE ontology into two composable layers of decoding-time constraints.\nOur two-layer architecture reflects the observation that structural violations (malformed output, unknown types) and semantic violations (type-constraint breaches) require fundamentally different enforcement mechanisms.\nThe \\emph{grammar layer} enforces JSON well-formedness and vocabulary validity via XGrammar.\nThe \\emph{semantic layer} maintains a stateful entity registry during generation: as entities are decoded, the registry records type assignments; when relation arguments are generated, dynamic logit masking restricts output to types compatible with the relation's domain/range specification.\nThe layers compose via bitwise AND with an \\emph{enabling dependency}: the grammar layer guarantees vocabulary membership, so the semantic layer can look up domain/range rules.\nUnlike type-constrained code generation where types are statically declared, IE requires a dynamic entity registry built during generation, with constraint targets that depend on the co-occurring relation.\nOn SciERC, OntoCoDec reduces DR violations from 15.37\\% to 0.93\\% ($\\downarrow$94\\%) while remaining F1-neutral ($+$0.13\\,pp).\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item The first systematic study of ontological constraint violations in LLM-based IE (spanning per-relation tightness analysis, dual-factor characterization, and post-hoc baseline comparison), violation rates are driven by constraint tightness rather than ontology size, and prompt-level intervention is per-relation unreliable.\n    \\item OntoCoDec, a two-layer compiled constraint framework that is the first to enforce pair-based domain/range type constraints via compiled stateful logit masks during autoregressive IE.\n    Unlike type-constrained decoding in code generation, where output types are statically determined by the grammar, IE requires stateful cross-field constraints because entity type validity depends on the co-occurring relation within each triple.\n    \\item Empirical validation on 3 models $\\times$ 3 datasets (2 RE + 1 EE): DR $\\downarrow$76--99\\% on SciERC and $\\downarrow$42--90\\% on DocRED with significant R-F1 gains (Qwen $p{<}0.001$, Llama $p{=}0.013$); cross-task generalization to WikiEvents (vocabulary violations $\\to$\\,0\\%; Entity F1 0\\%\\,$\\to$\\,12--18\\%); composability with LoRA (DR\\,$\\leq$\\,0.20\\% at $<$0.6\\,pp F1 cost).\n    \\item Characterization of the CF$\\leftrightarrow$DR trade-off inherent in per-slot enforcement (resolved by fine-tuning to CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.2\\%); beam search ($k{=}4$) as an orthogonal path that eliminates all four violation types under fine-tuned beam decoding (SciERC, Qwen3-8B) and reduces DR to 0\\% for calibrated models in zero-shot settings.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
        "numLines": 34,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 34
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../artifacts/_project/paper/section1_introduction_draft.tex"

        "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nWe present \\textsc{OntoCoDec}, which compiles Information Extraction (IE) ontology schemas into two composable token-level decoding constraints that provide formal guarantees during autoregressive generation:\n(1)~a \\emph{grammar layer} (XGrammar context-free grammar) guaranteeing JSON structural validity and entity/relation vocabulary membership (virtually eliminating entity type and relation vocabulary violations); and\n(2)~a \\emph{semantic layer} guaranteeing per-triple domain/range type compliance via a stateful entity registry and dynamic logit masking.\nThe two layers compose via bitwise AND on logit masks, with the grammar layer as an enabling prerequisite.\n%\nWithout constraints, Large Language Model (LLM) based IE produces systematic domain/range (DR) violations: 15.37\\% on SciERC (7 relations) and 18.46\\% on DocRED (96 relations), comparable rates governed by \\emph{constraint tightness} rather than ontology scale.\n\\textsc{OntoCoDec} reduces DR by 42--99\\% across three model families (Qwen3-8B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B) with no significant F1 cost.\nFor the primary model (Qwen3-8B): SciERC $+$0.13\\,pp ($p{=}0.58$), DocRED $+$0.50\\,pp ($p{<}0.001$); other models show comparable or larger gains (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n% exp_ids: phase2_scierc_full_s1, phase2_docred_full_s1, llama_docred_full_s1, mistral_docred\nFor calibrated models, residual DR correlates with the product of schema tightness and baseline violation rate, consistent with predictable constraint effectiveness from schema and model characteristics.\nComposed with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) fine-tuning, DR reaches $\\leq$\\,0.09\\% (SciERC) and 0.20\\% (DocRED) with relation F1 within $\\pm$0.6\\,pp, a near-lossless composition.\nWith beam search ($k{=}4$), the full stack eliminates all four violation types under fine-tuned beam decoding (SciERC, Qwen3-8B) while preserving extraction quality (R-F1 change ${<}$0.5\\,pp); in zero-shot beam decoding, DR reaches 0\\% for calibrated models on both RE datasets.\nThe framework generalizes to event extraction (WikiEvents: 56 event types, 80 roles) without architectural modification and eliminates event-vocabulary and role-vocabulary violations ($\\to$ 0\\%); where unconstrained outputs are severely off-schema, format compliance additionally enables entity extraction (Entity F1: 0\\%\\,$\\to$\\,12--18\\%).\nPer-relation independent enforcement introduces a cross-field consistency (CF) vs.\\ DR trade-off: CF violations arise (up to 12\\% in zero-shot) as the cost of per-slot domain/range guarantees; fine-tuning resolves this tension (CF\\,$\\leq$\\,1.2\\%).\n\\end{abstract}\n",
        "numLines": 17,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 17
      }
    },

      "- Explore: Fast read-only search agent for locating code. Use it to find files by pattern (eg. \"src/components/**/*.tsx\"), grep for symbols or keywords (eg. \"API endpoints\"), or answer \"where is X defined / which files reference Y.\" Do NOT use it for code review, design-doc auditing, cross-file consistency checks, or open-ended analysis — it reads excerpts rather than whole files and will miss content past its read window. SciERC schema:
- Entity types: Generic, Task, Method, Metric, Material, OtherScientificTerm
- Relations and domain/range constraints (from memory of prior analysis):
  - Used-for: broad constraints (Task, Method, Material, etc.)
  - Feature-of: broad
  - Hyponym-of: same-type pairs
  - Part-of: same-type pairs
  - Compare: Method-Method, Task-Task (tight constraint)
  - Conjunction: same-type pairs
  - Evaluate-for: Metric → Method/Task (tight constraint)

I need to check for three types of violations: relation types not in the schema, entity types not in the schema, and domain/range mismatches between head and tail types. Let me use grep to efficiently count what entity and relation types actually appear in the unconstrained output.

The valid entity types are Generic, Task, Method, Metric, Material, and OtherScientificTerm, while the valid relations are Used-for, Feature-of, Hyponym-of, Part-of, Compare, Conjunction, and Evaluate-for.

_source line 4222 · 2026-05-18T13:36:39.291Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
`"head":` appears on 540 lines. Since each line is a document (551 docs), this gives the number of documents that have at least one relation. But I need the TOTAL number of relation instances, not lines with relations.

The issue is that grep -c counts lines, not occurrences. A line can have multiple `"head":` occurrences (from both raw_output and parsed_output, and possibly gold_relations).

Let me think about this differently. Each document's JSONL line contains:
- gold_relations (with "head")
- raw_output (could contain "head")  


- parsed_output.relations (with "head")

I need to count only the parsed_output relations. This is tricky because grep alone can't easily isolate just that field. I could try using grep -o to count all occurrences of the pattern, but without piping I'm limited in what I can do. Let me see if there's a way to count matches per line and sum them up, though that's getting complicated without more sophisticated tools.

Actually, let me step back and look at the violation_checker.py file to understand how it calculates violations in the first place. That'll give me a better sense of what to expect when the agent runs it. I'm also realizing that "head" appears in multiple places within each JSONL line—in the gold_relations, as part of the raw_output string, and in parsed_output.relations—so a simple grep count isn't going to isolate just the relation triples I care about. Let me instead spot-check a few specific documents to manually verify some DR violations rather than trying to count everything programmatically.

Looking at that unconstrained relation from doc X96-1059_sent2, I need to check what the ontology constraints actually allow for Hyponym-of relationships.
